\begin{abstract}
Let $D(N)$ be the largest cardinality of a subset of $\{1,\ldots,N\}$
containing no nonzero square difference. We give a computer-assisted construction
proving $D(N)\ge N^{0.75806746-o(1)}$. This improves the exponent
$0.752796455874514\ldots$ in Krachun's ranked-block construction.
Our local objects are residue sets carrying intervals ordered along modular
square differences. A finite stopping-word argument shows that a surplus of
interval-moment contributions produces an asymptotic lower bound.
We combine six prime-chain alphabets, two composite alphabets with three
restricted digits in each prime coordinate, and a 25-state binary recursion.
All finite witnesses and the exact verification programs are embedded in the
\LaTeX\ source and attached to this PDF. The proof includes the lifting and
recursion arguments, the stopping-word criterion, and the justification of
the finite checking procedures. The numerical conclusion is computer-assisted;
the accompanying Lean 4 development formalizes the full bound, including all
finite certificate conditions.
\end{abstract}
\clearpage

\section{Introduction}
\subsection{The problem and the quantitative landscape}
For a positive integer $N$, put
\[
 D(N)=\max\bigl\{\card A:A\subseteq\{1,\ldots,N\},\quad
 (A-A)\cap\{1,4,9,\ldots\}=\varnothing\bigr\}.
\]
Furstenberg and S\'ark\"ozy independently proved that $D(N)=o(N)$
\cite{Furstenberg1977,Sarkozy1978}. The quantitative upper-bound problem has
subsequently developed through the work of Pintz, Steiger and Szemer\'edi,
Bloom and Maynard, and Green and Sawhney
\cite{PintzSteigerSzemeredi1988,BloomMaynard2022,GreenSawhney2025}.
In particular, Green and Sawhney prove a bound of the form
$D(N)\ll N\exp(-c\sqrt{\log N})$ for an absolute $c>0$
\cite[Theorem 1.1]{GreenSawhney2025}.
Our concern is the complementary construction problem: to maximize $\alpha$
in $D(N)\ge N^{\alpha-o(1)}$. All logarithms in this paper are natural.
The displayed notation means that, for every $\varepsilon>0$, the bound
$D(N)\ge N^{\alpha-\varepsilon}$ holds for every sufficiently large $N$.

Ruzsa's construction gives exponent
\[
 \frac12+\frac{\log7}{2\log65}=0.733077\ldots
\]
by restricting alternating base-$65$ digits and leaving the others free
\cite{Ruzsa1984}. Beigel and Gasarch, and independently Lewko, use a
12-element square-difference-free residue set modulo $205$ to obtain
\[
 \frac12+\frac{\log12}{2\log205}=0.7334117970\ldots
 \qquad\cite{BeigelGasarch2008,Lewko2015}.
\]
For this construction family, a conjectural restriction on the size of
square-difference-free sets modulo square-free moduli gives a natural
three-quarter barrier; it is not an upper bound on $D(N)$
\cite{Ruzsa1984,Lewko2015}.
Further bounds for square-avoiding sets modulo square-free integers are
studied by Gabdullin \cite{Gabdullin2018}. These address local square
avoidance, whereas the local objects here permit directed square edges.
Computational exploration by Georgiev, G\'omez-Serrano, Tao and Wagner
recovered the modulus-$205$ construction without improving its exponent
\cite[Section 16]{GeorgievEtAl2025}; this records a search outcome, not
an optimality theorem.

In the function-field analogue, Naslund \cite[Conjecture~13]{Naslund2022}
conjectured that square-difference-free sets of polynomials of degree below
$n$ over $\mathbb F_q$ have size at most $q^{3n/4}$, for odd $q$ and $4\mid n$.
Jones \cite{JonesPalomar2026} disproves this at $q=3$ for every $n\ge8$
divisible by $4$, with a Lean-verified construction registered in Palomar.

Krachun crossed the integer three-quarter barrier by allowing modular square differences, provided
that they consistently decrease a rank, and then combining the ranks over
coprime moduli \cite{Krachun2026}. His exponent is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:krachun}
 \alpha_{\mathrm K}=
 \frac{10+\sum_{i=1}^{10}\log p_i/\log t_i}
      {1+2\sum_{i=1}^{10}\log p_i/\log t_i}
 =0.752796455874514\ldots,
\end{equation}
where
\[
 (p_i,t_i)=(3,2),(7,3),(11,4),(19,5),(23,5),
 (31,7),(43,7),(59,9),(71,9),(103,11).
\]
Here $t_i$ is the size of a specified transitive sequence in the Paley
tournament modulo $p_i$. Bounds for such transitive subtournaments explain
some limitations of this particular prime-chain family
\cite{Satake2021,Krachun2026}; those limitations do not apply to all ordered
interval alphabets.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:main}
Let $\al=37903373/50000000=0.75806746$. For every $\varepsilon>0$ there is
$N_0(\varepsilon)$ such that, for every integer $N\ge N_0(\varepsilon)$,
there exists $A\subseteq\{1,\ldots,N\}$ with no nonzero square difference and
\[
 \card A\ge N^{\al-\varepsilon}.
\]
\end{theorem}

Relative to \eqref{eq:krachun}, the increase in the exponent is approximately
$0.005271004125486$. The comparison concerns the published/preprint
construction just described, not an upper bound on the true optimum.
Intermediate computations in the working research archive reached
$0.7580419$ and $0.758043893$; these are stages of the present research,
not additional bibliographic antecedents. The certificates used here were
newly saved and do not depend on any missing historical computational files.

\subsection{How the improvement works}
The ranked-block realization and CRT gluing steps are already present in
Krachun's Lemmas 4 and 5 \cite{Krachun2026}. We prove them below with the
opposite rank convention: a modular square difference increases the rank,
while the chosen integer representative decreases with rank.
The additional flexibility has three concrete sources.

First, a digit is assigned an interval $[a_x,a_x+w_x]$ rather than just a
rank. A square edge $x\to y$ requires $a_x+w_x\le a_y$, but incomparable
digits may share substantial portions of their intervals. Unequal widths
permit nonuniform digit frequencies. The useful statistic is the moment
$\sum_xw_x^f$, rather than just the number of digits or the unweighted
height of their graph. This is closely related to weighted chain constraints
in a partially ordered set \cite{Stanley1986} and to the familiar
entropy--log-sum identity \cite{CoverThomas2006}; neither theory is needed
as a black box in our proof.

Second, the odd components are built modulo $215^6$ and $437^6$, grouping
primes as $215=5\cdot43$ and $437=19\cdot23$. Each prime coordinate has
three restricted low digits, interleaved with free digits. Which position
first differs is determined separately at each prime. Conditional choices
of higher digits and changes to child supports reduce unnecessary interval
constraints. We certify the resulting explicit supports directly, so their
search histories are unnecessary for the proof.

Third, the prime $2$ supplies a component through a recursion whose states
record separate windows for even and odd digits. Four residue branches,
reflection, and parity changes allow a useful moment growth rate. The
recursion uses only 25 states and 94 branches, although the final alphabet
is taken at the fixed depth $10^{10}$. It is represented by induction, never
by an explicit list of all its digits.

The common principle is an interval-moment criterion. If alphabets in
pairwise coprime square bases $b_i$ satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:introcriterion}
 \sum_{x\in C_i}w_{i,x}^{f_i}\ge b_i^\alpha,
 \qquad f_i\ge0,\qquad \sum_i f_i>\alpha,
\end{equation}
then $\alpha$ is attainable. We prove this by stopping interval words at a
common width and selecting a single word length in each component. This
finite argument avoids asymptotic composition estimates. For our nine
components every $f_i$ is an exact rational, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:introsurplus}
 \sum_i f_i-\al=\frac{25671}{10^{12}}>0.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Scope and self-contained computational evidence}
Theorem~\ref{thm:main} is a computer-assisted theorem. Its analytic and
combinatorial implications are proved in the text; specific finite rational
inequalities are established by the supplied executable certificates.
No optimization output, floating-point eigenvalue, or search optimality
claim is an assumption of the proof.

The \LaTeX\ source contains a complete Python attachment, including all
witnesses and checking programs. Compiling that one source file writes the
attachment and embeds it in the PDF. Section~\ref{sec:verification} specifies
its data and acceptance conditions, and Appendix~\ref{app:reproduction}
gives extraction and execution instructions. Thus neither an external
research note nor an online data download is needed to reconstruct the
construction. The large raw integer arrays are distributed as an attachment,
not as thousands of typeset rows. Standard Python 3 and ordinary exact
integer/rational arithmetic suffice for verification.

\section{Ranked residues and ordered interval alphabets}\label{sec:ranked}
For $b\ge1$, let
$\QR(b)=\{z^2\bmod b:z\in\Z\}$, including zero. Residues are represented
canonically in $\{0,\ldots,b-1\}$. For distinct $x,y$ write $x\to_b y$ if
$y-x\in\QR(b)$. For composite $b$, a nonzero square residue may vanish
in some prime coordinates; invertibility is never imposed.

\begin{definition}
A ranked alphabet modulo $b$ is a finite set $C$ of canonical residues
with an integer function $r:C\to\{0,\ldots,h-1\}$, for some $h\ge1$,
such that $x\to_b y$ implies $r(x)<r(y)$.
An ordered interval alphabet assigns to every $x\in C$ an interval
$[a_x,a_x+w_x]\subseteq[0,1]$, with $w_x>0$, such that
$x\to_b y$ implies $a_x+w_x\le a_y$.
Its moment of order $f\ge0$ is $Z_f(C,w)=\sum_{x\in C}w_x^f$.
\end{definition}

Intervals may overlap when the corresponding digits are incomparable. The
graph of an ordered interval alphabet is acyclic, since a directed cycle
would imply that the sum of its positive widths is at most zero.

\begin{lemma}[Path budgets]\label{lem:paths}
Let a finite directed acyclic graph have positive vertex weights $w_x$.
There are intervals in $[0,1]$ of those widths ordered along every edge
if and only if the sum of the weights on every directed path is at most one.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Ordered intervals along a path have disjoint interiors, so their widths
sum to at most one. Conversely, let $d_x$ be the maximum weight of a path
ending at $x$, including $x$. Set $a_x=d_x-w_x$. Then $a_x\ge0$ and
$a_x+w_x=d_x\le1$. If $x\to y$, appending $y$ to a maximizing path ending
at $x$ gives $d_y\ge d_x+w_y$, whence $a_y\ge a_x+w_x$.
\end{proof}
This is the weighted-chain viewpoint underlying the chain polytope of a
finite poset \cite{Stanley1986}; the elementary proof above supplies the
part used here.

\begin{lemma}[Reverse-rank realization]\label{lem:reverse}
A ranked alphabet of size $q$ modulo $M$, with ranks in
$\{0,\ldots,h-1\}$, gives a square-difference-free set of size $q$ in
$\{1,\ldots,Mh\}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For the canonical residue $x$, choose
\[
 T(x)=1+x+M(h-1-r(x)).
\]
These numbers lie in the stated interval and are distinct modulo $M$.
If $T(y)-T(x)=z^2>0$, then $x\ne y$ and reduction modulo $M$ implies
$r(y)-r(x)\ge1$. Consequently
\[
 T(y)-T(x)=y-x-M(r(y)-r(x))\le(M-1)-M<0,
\]
a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[CRT gluing]\label{lem:crt}
Suppose $M_1,\ldots,M_n$ are pairwise coprime and $C_i$ is a ranked
alphabet modulo $M_i$, with $q_i$ digits and ranks below $h_i$.
Their CRT product is a ranked alphabet modulo $M=\prod_iM_i$, with
$Q=\prod_iq_i$ digits and ranks below
\[
 h=1+\sum_{i=1}^n(h_i-1).
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The Chinese remainder map identifies residues modulo $M$ with tuples of
residues modulo the $M_i$. Explicitly, writing $P_i=M/M_i$, choose
$u_i$ with $P_iu_i\equiv1\pmod{M_i}$ and take the canonical residue of
$\sum_i x_iP_iu_i$. This proves existence; divisibility by every $M_i$
proves uniqueness. Assign rank $r(x)=\sum_ir_i(x_i)$. A square difference
modulo $M$ is a square in each coordinate. Equal coordinates leave the rank
unchanged and unequal ones increase it. At least one coordinate is unequal,
so the sum strictly increases. The size and rank bounds follow.
\end{proof}
Lemmas~\ref{lem:reverse} and \ref{lem:crt} express the ranked realization
and gluing mechanism of \cite[Lemmas 4--5]{Krachun2026} in our convention.

\begin{lemma}[Square-base words]\label{lem:words}
Let $b=s^2>1$. If digits $C$ form a ranked alphabet with rank height $h$,
then their length-$k$ words form a ranked alphabet modulo $b^k$ with
$\card C^k$ digits and rank height $h^k$. If the digits instead carry
ordered intervals, the word intervals obtained by affine composition are
ordered along modular square differences as well.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Encode a word by $X=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}x_jb^j$. Uniqueness of the base-$b$
expansion gives injectivity. Suppose $Y-X\equiv z^2\pmod{b^k}$ and $X\ne Y$.
Let $j$ be the first position at which $x_j\ne y_j$, reading from the
least significant digit. Since $s^{2j}=b^j$ divides $z^2$, we have
$s^j\mid z$: this follows by comparing the valuations of every prime
dividing $s$. Dividing the congruence by $b^j$ and reducing modulo $b$
gives $y_j-x_j\equiv(z/s^j)^2\pmod b$, a nonzero square edge.

In the ranked case assign the word rank
$R(X)=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}r(x_j)h^{k-1-j}$. The first differing digit increases
its rank, and its place value dominates every possible decrease in the
remaining places. Thus $R(Y)>R(X)$. Its range is $0\le R<h^k$.

For intervals, put $F_x(t)=a_x+w_xt$ and assign
$F_{x_0}\circ\cdots\circ F_{x_{k-1}}([0,1])$. At the first differing
position, the two digit intervals are ordered. The remaining compositions
lie inside those digit intervals, and the common preceding maps are
increasing. This preserves the required order. The width is
$W_X=\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}w_{x_j}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Rounding intervals to ranks]\label{lem:round}
If every interval in an ordered alphabet has width at least $\gamma>0$,
then $r(x)=\lfloor a_x/\gamma\rfloor$ is a valid rank with
$0\le r(x)<\lceil1/\gamma\rceil$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
On an edge $a_y\ge a_x+\gamma$, so $r(y)\ge r(x)+1$.
Moreover $0\le a_x\le1-\gamma$, which gives the stated upper bound.
\end{proof}

\section{A finite interval-moment criterion}\label{sec:criterion}
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:criterion}
Let $n\ge1$. For each $i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$ let $C_i$ be an ordered
interval alphabet in a square base $b_i>1$, with the bases pairwise coprime.
Suppose there are common constants $0<\sigma\le\rho<1$ such that
$\sigma\le w_{i,x}\le\rho$ for all digits. If $\alpha\ge0$, $f_i\ge0$,
and
\[
 Z_{f_i}(C_i,w_i)\ge b_i^\alpha\quad(1\le i\le n),
 \qquad F:=\sum_{i=1}^nf_i>\alpha,
\]
then $D(N)\ge N^{\alpha-o(1)}$.
\end{theorem}
The common width bounds are automatic for a fixed finite collection of
nonempty alphabets with every width strictly between zero and one. They need
not be uniform over a sequence of proposed constructions.

\begin{lemma}[Stopping and selecting a length]\label{lem:stopping}
Fix one alphabet in base $b$, widths $\sigma\le w_x\le\rho<1$, and
$f\ge0$. Put $q=b^\alpha$ and suppose $Z=\sum_xw_x^f\ge q>0$.
For every integer $K\ge1$, with $\delta=\rho^K$, there exist
$1\le k\le K$ and a set $D$ of length-$k$ words such that
\[
 \sigma\delta<W_X\le\delta\quad(X\in D),\qquad
 q^k\le(K+1)\card D\,\delta^f.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Start with the empty word of width one. Extend each active word by all
digits; a child is active if its width exceeds $\delta$, and terminal
otherwise. Only active words are extended. No active word survives depth
$K$, since all depth-$K$ widths are at most $\rho^K=\delta$.
A terminal word has a parent wider than $\delta$, so its own width is
strictly greater than $\sigma\delta$.

Let $A_j$ be the sum of $W^f$ over active depth-$j$ words, and let $T_j$
be the sum over terminal words newly created at depth $j+1$. Multiplication
of widths gives the exact identities
\[
 A_0=1,\qquad A_K=0,\qquad A_{j+1}+T_j=Z A_j\quad(0\le j<K).
\]
If $T_j<q^{j+1}/(K+1)$ for every $j<K$, then induction, using $Z\ge q$,
gives
\[
 A_j\ge \frac{K+1-j}{K+1}q^j\quad(0\le j\le K).
\]
Indeed, subtracting $T_j$ from $ZA_j$ proves the next inequality, strictly
when $j+1>0$. At $j=K$ this contradicts $A_K=0$. Choose a $j$ for which
$T_j\ge q^{j+1}/(K+1)$ and retain precisely those terminal words; set
$k=j+1$. Since $f\ge0$ and $W\le\delta$, their moment is at most
$\card D\delta^f$. This proves the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:criterion}]
Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:stopping} separately in each component with the same
$K$ and $\delta=\rho^K$. Obtain lengths $k_i\in[1,K]$ and word sets $D_i$.
Their moduli $b_i^{k_i}$ are coprime perfect squares, and
Lemmas~\ref{lem:words} and \ref{lem:round} give ranks below
$H=\lceil1/(\sigma\delta)\rceil$ in every component.
Put $M=\prod_ib_i^{k_i}$ and $Q=\prod_i\card D_i$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:crt} gives a ranked alphabet of size $Q$ modulo $M$ with
height $h=1+n(H-1)$. Multiplying the selected-moment estimates yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:productmoment}
 M^\alpha\le Q\delta^F(K+1)^n.
\end{equation}
Since $\delta\le1$, the fixed constant $c=(n+1)(1+\sigma^{-1})$ satisfies
$h\le c/\delta$. Consequently
\[
 (Mh)^\alpha\le Qc^\alpha(K+1)^n\delta^{F-\alpha}.
\]
Because $F-\alpha>0$ and $0<\rho<1$, the last factor
$c^\alpha(K+1)^n\rho^{K(F-\alpha)}$ tends to zero.
For example, the ratio of consecutive terms tends to
$\rho^{F-\alpha}<1$, which proves this without any asymptotic counting
formula. Choose and fix $K$ so that $(Mh)^\alpha\le Q$.

Write $L=Mh>1$. Repeating this one ranked alphabet $t$ times and applying
Lemmas~\ref{lem:words} and \ref{lem:reverse} gives $Q^t$ square-difference-free
integers in $[1,L^t]$. For any $N\ge1$, let $t$ be the integer with
$L^t\le N<L^{t+1}$. Then
\[
 N^\alpha\le L^{(t+1)\alpha}\le Q^{t+1},\qquad
 D(N)\ge Q^t\ge Q^{-1}N^\alpha.
\]
For every $\varepsilon>0$, $N^\varepsilon\ge Q$ eventually, giving
$D(N)\ge N^{\alpha-\varepsilon}$ for every sufficiently large $N$.
All alphabets, $K$, and hence $L,Q$ have been fixed before $N$ varies.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Relation to entropy]\label{rem:entropy}
The criterion also explains the search objective. For a positive probability
vector $\pi$ on the digits, define
$H(\pi)=-\sum_x\pi_x\log\pi_x$ and
$R(\pi)=-\sum_x\pi_x\log w_x$. With
$\nu_x=w_x^f/Z_f$, the inequality $\log u\le u-1$ gives
$\sum_x\pi_x\log(\nu_x/\pi_x)\le0$. Thus
\[
 H(\pi)-fR(\pi)\le\log Z_f,
\]
with equality when $\pi=\nu$. This is the entropy variational identity
\cite{CoverThomas2006}. Equal-width chains are a special case, whereas
unequal widths exploit the actual partial order. The stopping argument
proves the criterion directly and needs neither this identity nor Stirling's
formula.
\end{remark}

\section{Odd-prime digit constructions}\label{sec:odd}
\subsection{Prime chains}
\begin{lemma}\label{lem:chain}
Let $p\equiv3\pmod4$ be prime and let $(s_0,\ldots,s_{t-1})$ be distinct
residues such that $s_j-s_i$ is a nonzero square modulo $p$ whenever $i<j$.
Then the $pt$ digits $s_j+pu$, $0\le u<p$, form an ordered interval
alphabet modulo $p^2$, with interval $[j/t,(j+1)/t]$ on each digit
$s_j+pu$. Its moment is $pt^{1-f}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The element $-1$ is not a square modulo $p$: otherwise an element of order
four would exist in the multiplicative group of order $p-1$, contradicting
$4\nmid p-1$. A square difference between different low digits therefore
points from the earlier to the later chain entry. If the low digits agree,
a nonzero difference has valuation one, and cannot be a square modulo $p^2$.
The intervals are ordered in the first case and no edge is possible in the
second. There are $p$ free copies of each interval, giving the moment.
\end{proof}
This is the local Paley-chain mechanism of \cite{Krachun2026}; the
alternating free-digit principle goes back to \cite{Ruzsa1984}.

\subsection{Separate first differences at different primes}
Fix distinct odd primes $p_1,\ldots,p_s$ and positive integers
$e_1,\ldots,e_s$. A low point $a$ consists of words
$(a_{p,0},\ldots,a_{p,e_p-1})$ with $0\le a_{p,j}<p$.
For distinct low points $a,b$, impose an edge $a\rightsquigarrow b$ if,
for every prime $p$, either the coordinate words are identical or their
first differing position $j$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lowedge}
 b_{p,j}-a_{p,j}\in\QR(p)\setminus\{0\}.
\end{equation}
The first differing position is allowed to depend on $p$. No comparison is
required from a prime whose entire low word is unchanged.

\begin{lemma}[Prime-coordinate free-digit lift]\label{lem:odd}
Suppose a finite set $S$ of distinct low points has positive intervals in
$[0,1]$ ordered along every edge $\rightsquigarrow$.
Put $F=\prod_pp^{e_p}$. For each $a\in S$, choose arbitrary
$u_{p,j}\in\{0,\ldots,p-1\}$ and form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:oddlift}
 x_p=\sum_{j=0}^{e_p-1}
       \bigl(a_{p,j}p^{2j}+u_{p,j}p^{2j+1}\bigr)\pmod{p^{2e_p}}.
\end{equation}
Combining the $x_p$ by CRT produces an ordered interval alphabet in base
$F^2$, with $F\card S$ digits. Every free copy uses its low point's interval,
and the moment is $F\sum_{a\in S}w_a^f$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Unique prime-adic digit expansions and CRT show that all these digits are
distinct and count their number. Suppose different lifted digits $x,y$
have a square difference modulo $F^2$. Consider a prime coordinate in which
they differ. If $k<2e_p$ is the first differing actual digit position, then
$y_p-x_p$ is divisible by $p^k$ but not by $p^{k+1}$: all lower digits
agree and the coefficient at $p^k$ is nonzero modulo $p$.
The congruence to a square forces $k$ to be even. Indeed, if
$z^2\equiv y_p-x_p\pmod{p^{2e_p}}$, the valuation of $z^2$ below
$2e_p$ must equal $k$, so $k=2j$; dividing shows that the leading nonzero
difference is a square modulo $p$.

If the low coordinate words differ, the first actual difference must thus
occur at their first differing low digit, and \eqref{eq:lowedge} holds.
If two distinct lifted digits have the same low point in every coordinate,
some first actual difference would instead lie in a free, odd position,
a contradiction. Thus their low points are distinct, and each coordinate
is either identical as a low word or obeys \eqref{eq:lowedge}. The assumed
low interval order therefore gives the required lifted order. Counting
free copies gives the moment formula.
\end{proof}

A single common first-difference index across all prime coordinates would
impose extra edges that this proof does not require. This distinction lets
us use conditional higher low digits and different child supports.
Only the final set of points, not a proposed recursive description of how
it was discovered, is an input to Lemma~\ref{lem:odd}.

\section{A binary recursion with parity windows}\label{sec:binary}
\subsection{States and transitions}
A state $s$ has an even window $E_s$ and an odd window $O_s$ in $[0,1]$.
A window may be absent; each present window is a closed interval of
positive length, and at least one window is present. At depth $m$, the
state will describe an ordered interval alphabet modulo $4^m$, with the
interval of each digit inside the window of its parity.

A transition from $s$ has at most one branch for each $r\in\{0,1,2,3\}$.
A branch specifies a child $j$, scale $u>0$, shift $t\in\R$, and bits
$\tau,\eta\in\{0,1\}$, called swap and reflect. For a child digit $x$
modulo $Q=4^{m-1}$, let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:binarydigit}
 x'=((-1)^\eta x+\tau)\bmod Q,\qquad y=r+4x'.
\end{equation}
When $\eta=1$ reflect the child interval $[a,a+w]$ to
$[1-a-w,1-a]$; then apply the increasing affine map $z\mapsto t+uz$.
The parity of $x'$ is the parity of $x$ toggled by $\tau$, since $Q$ is
even. For transformed parity $p\in\{0,1\}$, let $J_{r+4p}$ be the
placed window obtained from the child window of parity $p\mathbin{\mathrm{xor}}\tau$,
with the same reflection and affine map. An absent child window contributes
no $J_{r+4p}$.

The transition conditions are
\begin{align}
 J_{r+4p}&\subseteq E_s\quad(r\text{ even}),\qquad
 J_{r+4p}\subseteq O_s\quad(r\text{ odd}),\label{eq:containment}\\
 \sup J_k&\le\inf J_{k+1\bmod8}
 \quad\text{whenever both windows are present}.\label{eq:cyclic}
\end{align}
In particular, \eqref{eq:cyclic} includes the comparison from class $7$
to class $0$. Missing classes make such a cyclic specification feasible.

\begin{lemma}[Binary transition]\label{lem:binary}
For $m\ge2$, if the child states are valid at depth $m-1$ and
\eqref{eq:containment}--\eqref{eq:cyclic} hold, the branches construct a
valid state at depth $m$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Different branches give different residues modulo four, and within a
branch \eqref{eq:binarydigit} is injective. Thus the digits are distinct.
Containment of intervals follows from \eqref{eq:containment}.

Suppose $Y-X$ is a nonzero square modulo $4^m$. If the digits belong to
one branch, then $Y-X=4(y'-x')$ modulo $4^m$. Any corresponding square root
is even, so division by four gives a square difference modulo $Q$.
Without reflection the child order applies directly. With reflection the
transformed difference equals the negative of the original child
difference; consequently the child square edge has the opposite orientation.
Reflection of the intervals reverses that order once more, giving the
required transformed interval order. Translation cancels in differences,
and the positive scale preserves order.

For different branches whose residues differ by two modulo four, the
difference is $2$ modulo four and is not a square. In the remaining case
the difference is odd. Every odd square is $1$ modulo eight, since
$(2k+1)^2=4k(k+1)+1$. Therefore the numerical classes of $X,Y$ are
$k,k+1$ cyclically modulo eight. Their intervals lie in the corresponding
$J$ windows, so \eqref{eq:cyclic} orders them. This exhausts all cases.
Only the necessary condition on odd squares is used.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Moment growth and a finite depth}
Fix $0<f<1$. Let $v_s>0$ and $a>1$ satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rows}
 \sum_{\text{branches }s\to j}u^f v_j\ge a v_s\quad\text{for every }s.
\end{equation}
This positive-vector certificate is a familiar device for bounding the
growth of a nonnegative matrix \cite{Seneta2006}. Here the required
consequence is just the following elementary induction.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:growth}
Let $d_s$ be the length of the wider present window of state $s$, and put
$c=\min_s d_s/v_s>0$. The transitions construct depth-$m$ states with
moments $Z_{s,m}\ge ca^{m-1}v_s$ for all $m\ge1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
At depth one choose just the digit $0$ or $1$ of the parity of the wider
window and assign it the whole window. A singleton has no nontrivial
square edge. Its moment $d_s^f$ is at least $d_s$, since $0<d_s\le1$.
This gives $Z_{s,1}\ge cv_s$. Reflection and translation do not change
widths, scaling multiplies each moment by $u^f$, and different branches
are disjoint. Thus
\[
 Z_{s,m}=\sum_{s\to j}u^fZ_{j,m-1}
 \ge ca^{m-2}\sum_{s\to j}u^fv_j\ge ca^{m-1}v_s.
\]
Validity follows inductively from Lemma~\ref{lem:binary}.
\end{proof}

If a root state has both windows $[0,1]$ and $v_{\mathrm{root}}=1$, it
produces an unrestricted interval alphabet modulo $4^m$.
Shrinking every final interval at its left endpoint by a factor of $1/2$
preserves all orders, makes all widths at most $1/2$, and multiplies the
moment by $2^{-f}$. Therefore the condition
\begin{equation}\label{eq:binarymargin}
 \log c+(m-1)\log a-f\log2-\alpha m\log4>0
\end{equation}
implies the required moment $Z_f>(4^m)^\alpha$. This uses only a fixed
finite depth, even if its numerical value is enormous.

\section{The explicit nine components}\label{sec:parameters}
We now fix all the data used in Theorem~\ref{thm:main}. Every terminating
decimal in the construction and in its verification thresholds denotes an
exact rational. The six chains are as follows; all displayed forward
differences are nonzero squares modulo the indicated prime.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{rrl}\toprule
$p$&$t$&Ordered chain\\\midrule
3&2&$0,1$\\
7&3&$0,1,2$\\
11&4&$0,1,4,5$\\
31&7&$0,1,8,5,2,9,10$\\
59&9&$0,1,28,17,22,4,26,20,29$\\
103&11&$0,1,29,92,2,61,17,30,93,18,19$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
There are 244 ordered pairs of distinct entries in these six rows. The
finite checker verifies the square relation in both orientations for all
of them. Lemma~\ref{lem:chain} gives the corresponding prime alphabets.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{lll}\toprule
Component&Square base $b_i$&Exact moment power $f_i$\\\midrule
Chain at $3$&$3^2$&$0.181945506487$\\
Chain at $7$&$7^2$&$0.085799249226$\\
Chain at $11$&$11^2$&$0.107233269134$\\

\caption{The parameters used throughout the proof.}\label{tab:powers}
\end{table}

For a chain of length $t$ modulo $p$, the real number
$f_p^*=1-(2\al-1)\log p/\log t$ would give equality
$pt^{1-f_p^*}=p^{2\al}$. The table uses a rational rounded down to twelve
decimal places instead. Exact logarithm bounds certify that each displayed
$f_p$ lies in $(0,f_p^*)$ and hence gives the strict moment inequality
$pt^{1-f_p}>p^{2\al}$. The following are conservative rational lower
bounds for $(1-f_p)\log t-(2\al-1)\log p$:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{rrrrrr}\toprule
$p=3$&$p=7$&$p=11$&$p=31$&$p=59$&$p=103$\\\midrule
$1.98\cdot10^{-13}$&$7.60\cdot10^{-14}$&$8.09\cdot10^{-13}$&
$3.99\cdot10^{-13}$&$2.21\cdot10^{-13}$&$2.06\cdot10^{-12}$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\subsection{The two odd witnesses}
The attachment contains two explicit finite arrays. Their mathematical
format is described in Section~\ref{sec:verification}; these arrays are
part of the construction, not parameters to be supplied later.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lrr}\toprule
&$215$ component&$437$ component\\\midrule
Primes&$5,43$&$19,23$\\
Low digits per prime&3&3\\
Low points&4,913&19,683\\
Directed low edges&6,031,254&77,614,486\\
Width denominator&$10^{14}$&$10^{16}$\\
Free multiplicity&$215^3$&$437^3$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For the $215$ component, integer longest-path computation in the supplied
order gives maximum path weight
\[
 \frac{99999974386028}{10^{14}}<1.
\]
Lemma~\ref{lem:paths} therefore constructs the interval starts from the
listed widths. For the $437$ component the starts are supplied explicitly;
every directed edge satisfies its interval inequality and the largest
right endpoint is
\[
 \frac{9999999524038902}{10^{16}}<1.
\]
All widths are positive and strictly smaller than one. The low-point
moment inequalities have the certified margins
\begin{align}
 \log\sum_{a\in S_{215}}w_a^{f_{215}}
       -(2\al-1)\log(215^3)&>6.97840\cdot10^{-10},\label{eq:odd215margin}\\
 \log\sum_{a\in S_{437}}w_a^{f_{437}}
       -(2\al-1)\log(437^3)&>6.95598\cdot10^{-10}.\label{eq:odd437margin}
\end{align}
Lemma~\ref{lem:odd} multiplies the moments by the free multiplicities,
so the lifted alphabets satisfy $Z_{f_i}>b_i^{\al}$.

\subsection{The binary witness}
The attachment specifies 25 states, 94 branches and a positive rational
vector $v$. State indices start at zero; the root has index $1$, both its
windows are $[0,1]$, and $v_1=1$. The exact parameters are
\[
 f_B=0.154942497274,\quad
 a=\frac{1430118728343}{500000000000},\quad m=10^{10},\quad
 c=\frac{285516532458}{803818168703}.
\]
The finite geometry test checks 304 containment and cyclic-order
inequalities, including the wrap from class $7$ to class $0$.
It also checks all state, branch, and positivity requirements.
The growth-row and logarithmic margins are
\begin{align}
 \min_s\left(\sum_{s\to j}u^{f_B}v_j-av_s\right)
       &>6.99293\cdot10^{-9},\label{eq:rowmargin}\\
 \log a-\al\log4&>3.05203\cdot10^{-9},\label{eq:growthmargin}\\
 \log c+(m-1)\log a-f_B\log2-\al m\log4
       &>28.32697028.\label{eq:depthmargin}
\end{align}
The rational $c$ is checked by taking the minimum of $d_s/v_s$ over the
25 states. Lemmas~\ref{lem:binary} and \ref{lem:growth}, followed by final
halving of widths, give $Z_{f_B}>(4^m)^{\al}$.

\subsection{Closing the bound}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:main}]
The exact finite checks just specified are established by the executable
certificate and the checking procedures proved sound in
Section~\ref{sec:verification}. Lemmas~\ref{lem:chain}, \ref{lem:odd},
\ref{lem:binary} and \ref{lem:growth} turn the finite data into nine ordered
interval alphabets satisfying the moments in Table~\ref{tab:powers}.
All these alphabets are finite and nonempty, and all widths are in $(0,1)$.
In particular, the huge binary depth is fixed now. Taking the minimum and
maximum over the finitely many widths gives common
$0<\sigma\le w\le\rho<1$; no useful numerical lower bound on $\sigma$
is needed.

The prime supports are disjoint: the six chain primes, the pairs
$\{5,43\}$ and $\{19,23\}$, and the prime $2$. Thus the nine bases are
pairwise coprime perfect squares. Adding the exact rational powers gives
\[
 \sum_p f_p=0.508715800379,\qquad
 \sum_i f_i=0.758067485671=\al+\frac{25671}{10^{12}}.
\]
Every hypothesis of Theorem~\ref{thm:criterion} now holds with
$\alpha=\al$. That theorem supplies the conclusion for every sufficiently
large $N$, with all finite choices made before the limit.
\end{proof}

\section{Exact finite verification}\label{sec:verification}
\subsection{Data sufficient to define the construction}
The embedded archive contains the manifest and the three witness JSON files
listed in Appendix~\ref{app:hashes}, together with their checking programs.
The manifest fixes $\al$, both odd powers, the prime chains, and the
SHA-256 hashes of the three witnesses. The binary witness fixes its own
power, depth, vector and growth parameter. The rational chain check gives
the six powers in Table~\ref{tab:powers}. All parameters used by the proof
are compared exactly; numerical fields left over from discovery are ignored.

For an odd witness, \texttt{primes} is the ordered list of coordinate
primes and \texttt{depth} is the number of low digits in each coordinate.
The array \texttt{points} lists the low words; each entry consists of one
length-three word per prime, in increasing significance. A common positive
integer \texttt{width\_denominator} $D$ and positive integers
\texttt{width\_numerators} $n_j$ define $w_j=n_j/D$.
The $437$ file additionally gives \texttt{start\_numerators} $s_j$,
defining $a_j=s_j/D$. The $215$ starts are defined by
Lemma~\ref{lem:paths}. Thus the listed data specify every interval without
reference to the optimization programs.

In the binary witness, a state's window record is $[a,b,c,d,q]$ and means
$E_s=[a/q,b/q]$, $O_s=[c/q,d/q]$; the pair $c=d=-1$ denotes an absent
odd window. Every present window is checked to lie in $[0,1]$ with positive
length. Each branch lists its residue \texttt{r}, \texttt{child} index,
\texttt{swap} and \texttt{reflect} bits, and rational \texttt{scale} and
\texttt{shift} as numerator--denominator pairs. The vector entries are
rational strings. Equations~\eqref{eq:binarydigit}--\eqref{eq:rows} specify
precisely what this data means.

\subsection{Complete geometric checks}
The $215$ checker checks prime validity, distinctness of points, all digit
ranges and word lengths, positivity of the rational widths, and all
ordered distinct pairs. For each pair it evaluates \eqref{eq:lowedge}
separately in each coordinate. Every edge must point forward in the supplied
list. Writing $d_j$ for a path numerator, it then computes
\[
 d_j=n_j+\max\bigl(\{d_i:i\rightsquigarrow j\}\cup\{0\}\bigr)
\]
and checks $\max_jd_j\le D$. This is exactly the recurrence in
Lemma~\ref{lem:paths}; induction in the checked topological order proves
its validity. No claim about a transitive reduction is used.

For the larger $437$ witness the checker computes the identical full edge
relation with integer bitsets. Here is why this accelerated check is
complete. For one prime, let $G(v)$ be the bitset of source points whose
coordinate word begins with the prefix $v$. For a target word
$b=(b_0,\ldots,b_{e-1})$, its allowable sources have bitset
\begin{equation}\label{eq:prefix}
 G(b)\ \cup\ \bigcup_{j=0}^{e-1}\ \bigcup_{q\in\QR(p)\setminus\{0\}}
 G\bigl((b_0,\ldots,b_{j-1},b_j-q)\bmod p\bigr).
\end{equation}
The first term covers identical words; each remaining term fixes the first
difference and permits all subsequent digits. Thus \eqref{eq:prefix} is
exactly the relation required at that prime. Intersect the bitsets over
primes and remove the target itself. This enumerates every incoming edge.
The checker also verifies that every incoming index precedes its target.

The rational inequalities are checked without iterating through individual
bits. Sort vertices by end numerator $s_i+n_i$, sweep target starts in
increasing order, and maintain the bitset $B$ of vertices whose ends exceed
the current start. The incoming bitset must have empty intersection with
$B$. At each step $B$ has exactly that definition, so this is equivalent
to checking $s_i+n_i\le s_j$ on every edge $i\rightsquigarrow j$.
All sizes, ranges, distinctness, starts, widths and endpoints are checked
separately. The counts in Section~\ref{sec:parameters} are outputs of
these complete checks, not sampled tests.

The binary geometry checker constructs every $J$ window with exact
fractions and checks \eqref{eq:containment} and \eqref{eq:cyclic}, alongside
unique branch residues, valid child indices, positive scales, and valid
bits. Its 304 comparisons count two endpoint containments per present
placed window and one inequality per present adjacent pair.
Lemma~\ref{lem:binary} proves why these finitely many conditions imply
validity at arbitrary depth. A direct small-depth test is not a substitute
for that lemma and is unnecessary for the main verification.

\subsection{Rational logarithm enclosures}\label{sec:arithmetic}
All decisive numerical comparisons use rational bounds. We give the details
both to specify their meaning and to separate verification from discovery.
For $1\le x\le2$, put $z=(x-1)/(x+1)\in[0,1/3]$. Integration of the
geometric series, or subtraction of the series for $\log(1+z)$ and
$\log(1-z)$, gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:logseries}
 \log x=2\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{z^{2j+1}}{2j+1}+R_n,
 \qquad 0\le R_n\le
 \frac{2z^{2n+1}}{(2n+1)(1-z^2)}.
\end{equation}
The tail bound follows by replacing all its denominators by $2n+1$ and
summing the resulting geometric series. For any positive rational $x$,
write $x=2^k y$ with $1\le y\le2$ and use
$\log x=k\log2+\log y$. If $k<0$, the interval endpoints for $k\log2$
are reversed. Every series term and bound is rational.

The $215$ checker uses 32 terms of \eqref{eq:logseries} with exact
fractions, and the binary checker uses 70. The $437$ checker uses 48
terms with outward rounding at scale $S=2^{192}$. More explicitly, if
$L_z\le Sz\le U_z$, set
\[
 L_{z^2}=\lfloor L_z^2/S\rfloor,\qquad
 U_{z^2}=\lceil U_z^2/S\rceil.
\]
Starting from $L_z,U_z$, multiply these bounds successively, rounding every
lower product down and every upper product up. In summing the $j$th term,
round division by $2j+1$ in the same directions. After $n$ terms the
upper bound $U_{\mathrm{next}}$ for $S z^{2n+1}$ gives the additional
scaled tail bound
\[
 \left\lceil
 \frac{2U_{\mathrm{next}}S}{(2n+1)(S-U_{z^2})}
 \right\rceil.
\]
Here $S-U_{z^2}>0$, as follows from $z\le1/3$ and the displayed scale.
Induction preserves enclosure through every operation, proving that the
resulting interval contains the exact logarithm. These are standard
outward-rounding principles \cite{MooreKearfottCloud2009}, with the
specific error bound proved above.

For clarity, write the resulting rational endpoints as $\ell(x),u(x)$,
so $\ell(x)\le\log x\le u(x)$. A positive rational proposal $L$ for
$x^f$ is accepted as a lower bound only when
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poweraccept}
 u(L)\le f\ell(x),\qquad f\ge0.
\end{equation}
Then $\log L\le f\log x$, and hence $L\le x^f$. Similarly a proposed
upper endpoint $U$ is accepted only if $\ell(U)\ge fu(x)$.
Decimal arithmetic proposes candidates; it never decides the validity of
a power enclosure. A poor proposal causes rejection rather than a false
positive.

For the odd components the proposals are integer multiples of $10^{-24}$.
If $L_j\le w_j^f$ have been accepted, the checker proves
\[
 \ell\Bigl(\sum_jL_j\Bigr)
 -(2\al-1)e\sum_p u(p)>0.
\]
Since $2\al-1>0$, this proves the lifted moment inequality. Lower
bounds for the margins in \eqref{eq:odd215margin}--\eqref{eq:odd437margin}
are obtained from that same rational expression.
For the binary component, accepted lower powers give lower bounds on
$\sum u^fv_j-av_s$. The fixed-depth check uses
\[
 \ell(c)+(m-1)\ell(a)-f_Bu(2)-\al m u(4)>0.
\]
This proves \eqref{eq:depthmargin} directly, without approximating the
astronomically large alphabet. The chain check proves
$(1-f_p)\ell(t)-(2\al-1)u(p)>0$ for each of the six rational powers.
Finally \eqref{eq:introsurplus} is an exact rational addition.

\subsection{Independent arithmetic and the trust boundary}
An additional program in the attachment checks the nontrivial moment,
binary growth, and global arithmetic using a different expansion:
\[
 \log x=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{t^k}{k}+R_n',\quad
 t=1-1/x\in[0,1/2],\quad
 0\le R_n'\le\frac{t^{n+1}}{(n+1)(1-t)}
\]
for $1\le x\le2$. It uses 230 terms and outward-rounded integer bounds
with scale $10^{60}$; its power proposals use denominator $10^{40}$.
The same geometric-tail and induction arguments prove its enclosures.
This check does not replace the geometry checks. It provides independently
implemented arithmetic for the same pinned witnesses.

The complete supplied run accepts all the finite conditions above. This
is executable finite evidence: the correctness of the numerical theorem
uses execution of the supplied integer/rational programs, not just their
printed success messages. The elementary implications of the conditions
have been proved in the text. The code can be inspected independently of
its compressed storage by extracting the attachment.

A separate Lean 4 development proves Theorem~\ref{thm:criterion} and the
full numerical conclusion $D(N)\ge N^{0.75806746-o(1)}$, including all finite
certificate conditions. It formalizes the six prime-chain components, both
reconstructed composite alphabets, and the entire binary recursion, together
with their order and numerical moment checks. The proof uses only the usual
foundational axioms; no finite computation remains an unproved hypothesis.
Lean and Mathlib provide the proof infrastructure
\cite{deMouraUllrich2021,Mathlib2020}. The English argument above is complete
on its own and uses no Lean theorem as a premise.

\section{What the construction leaves open}\label{sec:discussion}
The proof isolates finite quantities that future searches can improve:
feasible low-point supports and their interval moments, the choice of
coprime prime groups, and the binary transition geometry. Increasing local
cardinality can be unhelpful if it forces more ordering or a worse width
profile. Likewise, a child support that performs worse on its own can be
useful when its interfaces to other children reduce the combined ordering
cost. Such observations motivate searches; the theorem depends only on
the final certified arrays.

The number $0.75806746$ is a certified feasible exponent, not an asserted
optimum for the method or for $D(N)$. In particular, the possible limitations
of prime Paley chains discussed in \cite{Krachun2026,Satake2021} do not
bound the composite and binary interval alphabets used here. The present
proof gives no construction of exponent $0.76$ and no new general upper
bound. Its very large fixed depths also make it unsuitable as a practical
construction for moderate $N$; the objective here is the asymptotic exponent.

bmFyeS1wdXNoL2luZGVwZW5kZW50X2FyaXRobWV0aWMucHlQSwECFAMUAAAACAAAACtdnwyDH44B
AADaAgAABgAAAAAAAAAAAAAAgAH66gUAcnVuLnB5UEsFBgAAAAAMAAwAMAQAAKzsBQAAAA==
"""

def main():
    if not __debug__:
        raise SystemExit('Run without -O or -OO: all verification assertions must execute.')
    parser=argparse.ArgumentParser(description=__doc__)
    parser.add_argument('--extract',type=pathlib.Path)
    args=parser.parse_args()
    raw=base64.b64decode(PAYLOAD)
    if hashlib.sha256(raw).hexdigest()!=ARCHIVE_SHA256:
        raise SystemExit('Certificate archive checksum mismatch.')
    def verify(destination):
        destination.mkdir(parents=True,exist_ok=True)
        with zipfile.ZipFile(io.BytesIO(raw)) as archive:
            for entry in archive.infolist():
                relative=pathlib.PurePosixPath(entry.filename)
                if relative.is_absolute() or '..' in relative.parts:
                    raise ValueError('Unsafe archive member')
                target=destination.joinpath(*relative.parts)
                if target.exists():
                    raise FileExistsError('Use a fresh extraction directory: '+str(target))
                target.parent.mkdir(parents=True,exist_ok=True)
                target.write_bytes(archive.read(entry))
        subprocess.run([sys.executable,str(destination/'run.py')],check=True)
    if args.extract:
        verify(args.extract.resolve())
    else:
        with tempfile.TemporaryDirectory(prefix='sarkozy-paper-') as directory:
            verify(pathlib.Path(directory))
if __name__=='__main__':
    main()
\end{filecontents*}

\section{Reproducing the entire finite verification}\label{app:reproduction}
The attachment is a self-contained Python program carrying a compressed
archive of the exact data and all programs needed by the checks.
\begin{center}
\textattachfile[color=0 0 0.5,mimetype=text/plain,description={Exact witnesses and complete verification programs}]{square-difference-free-certificate.py}{\fbox{\texttt{square-difference-free-certificate.py}}}
\end{center}
A PDF viewer supporting embedded files can save this attachment.
Alternatively, compiling the single \texttt{square-difference-free-sets-of-exponent-0.75806746.tex}
source with \texttt{pdflatex} writes that same Python file beside the build
outputs. The source contains the actual archive bytes, not a link to them.
Run
\begin{lstlisting}
python3 square-difference-free-certificate.py
\end{lstlisting}
The program checks the embedded archive hash, extracts into a temporary
directory, runs the complete geometry/moment verifier, runs the rational
chain-power check, and runs the independent arithmetic check. It makes no
network requests and needs no third-party Python packages. To retain the
extracted data, source code and newly generated reports, choose a fresh
output directory:
\begin{lstlisting}
python3 square-difference-free-certificate.py --extract certificate-check
\end{lstlisting}
The run finishes with
\begin{lstlisting}
PAPER CERTIFICATE PASS: complete geometry, rational target powers, and independent arithmetic.
\end{lstlisting}
The wrapper and complete entry points reject Python's \texttt{-O} and
\texttt{-OO} modes, because some constituent routines use assertions as
checks. Use the supplied entry point rather than bypassing those guards.
Compilation of the PDF does not execute these mathematical checks; they
are a separate explicit verification step.

The principal verification files in the extracted archive are:
\begin{itemize}\small\raggedright
\item \path{research-notes/improved-bound/verify_bound.py}: complete manifest,
component, chain, coprimality and global-budget checking;
\item \path{research-notes/odd-search/verify_candidate.py}: complete pairwise
low-code and longest-path checking, exact rational logarithms;
\item \path{research-notes/odd-push/verify_fast.py}: equivalent full-edge
bitset checking and directed fixed-point logarithms;
\item \path{research-notes/binary-search/check_geometry.py} and
\path{research-notes/binary-search/certify.py}: binary transition geometry,
positive-vector inequalities and fixed-depth moment;
\item \path{research-notes/lean-planning/rational_chain_powers.py}: the six
rational powers and the exact surplus in Table~\ref{tab:powers};
\item \path{research-notes/binary-push/independent_arithmetic.py}: independent
arithmetic using the second logarithm series.
\end{itemize}
The archive preserves the historical file names for reproducibility; they
do not introduce dependencies on any other files in the working repository.
Discovery-only functions present in some source modules are not executed.

\section{Certificate identity}\label{app:hashes}
The following SHA-256 hashes fix the exact bytes of the data files in the
embedded archive. Hashes identify witnesses; the finite checks, rather
than the hashes themselves, establish their mathematical properties.
\begin{description}
\item[Manifest:] \path{research-notes/capacity-bound/bound.json}\\
\nolinkurl{836b231c32e771dfaf494a74de40b5213c8f8606865ac8309724e6a1fc5ec491}
\item[Odd $215$:] \path{research-notes/odd-search/q215-depth3-product.json}\\
\nolinkurl{dae38a756de83c99d65973ff85f23a7068ce6e85a7a4f12400a923f24cf46574}
\item[Odd $437$:] \path{research-notes/odd-push/q437-depth3-final.json}\\
\nolinkurl{7abd0c007c42fa1a5484ea8a4d02c56b04898209f74a0403c5503c03395e58d3}
\item[Binary:] \path{research-notes/binary-push/certificate-alpha-0758067460.json}\\
\nolinkurl{4835df59b0cc8444abe274a2c0d923fa89d023687c52ec58a7c31a49a8b03c87}
\end{description}
The SHA-256 of the embedded ZIP archive is
\begin{center}\small
\nolinkurl{5dcaffc0c826e178ad9b7ec3b42007f92eb4f7f44a03fe9c24012247f87b1e6b}.
\end{center}
The attached program checks this identity before unpacking.
